I’ve had periods before where most days felt like that same heaviness—like everything was drained of color. I’d keep going because the house was busy, but I’d end up crying somewhere private, like the laundry room, because I didn’t want anyone to see how hopeless I felt.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period when you felt abnormally high, energized, or unusually irritable for several days?\n\nPatient: Yes. Earlier this month I had a stretch where I felt almost electrically upbeat, like I had all this energy. But then I’d snap at everyone over tiny things—like a medication cup being in the wrong place or the morning schedule changing. It didn’t feel like my usual self.\n\nDoctor: During that energized stretch, did you need much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes. I was only sleeping maybe three hours a night, and I’d still wake up before sunrise feeling wide awake and sharp, like I didn’t really need any more sleep.\n\nDoctor: During that energized stretch, did you feel unusually confident or powerful?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it powerful. I don’t really remember feeling like I was better than everyone or anything like that. Maybe more sure of myself than usual, but that part is hard to say.\n\nDoctor: During that energized stretch, were you more active than usual—starting lots of projects or doing much more than you normally would?\n\nPatient: Yes. For several days I just couldn’t sit still after work. I’d pace around the house, measure rooms, make lists, reorganize cabinets—stuff like that—and I’d keep doing it long after everyone else had gone to bed.\n\nDoctor: During that energized stretch, were you talking more than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t remember anyone telling me I was talking nonstop or anything like that. I may have been more chatty just because I felt keyed up, but I can’t say for sure.\n\nDoctor: During that energized stretch, did you do anything unusually risky or impulsive for you?\n\nPatient: Yes. I bought a bunch of things all at once—monitoring equipment, ramps, bedding, supplies—because at the time it felt like we absolutely needed them right then. Afterward I realized I’d put it all on the credit card, and we really can’t cover those charges.\n\nDoctor: During that energized stretch, did it cause major problems in your life—like you couldn’t function at work or home, or someone felt you needed emergency help?\n\nPatient: Yes, it’s caused real problems. I’ve been making mistakes at the clinic, which scares me, and I’ve been avoiding friends because I’m worried I’ll either snap at someone or just break down. At home, my husband has had to take over a lot—bills, meals, and watching out for me when my thoughts get really dark.\n\nDoctor: How many days did that energized and irritable stretch last?\n\nPatient: It lasted several days. I can’t remember the exact number, but for those days I couldn’t sit still after work—I was pacing around the house, measuring rooms, making lists, reorganizing cabinets, staying up doing that long after everyone else went to bed.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. That’s what scared me.\n\nDoctor: When those thoughts happen, do they feel unwanted and disturbing to you?\n\nPatient: Yes. They scare me. It’s not like I want to be thinking that way. I’ll have that thought that it would be easier if I didn’t wake up, or when I’m driving home I wonder what would happen if I just didn’t turn the wheel… and then I get really disturbed by the fact that I even thought it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever do anything to neutralize those thoughts when they happen?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I have a specific thing I do to cancel them out. Mostly I just get scared and try to move my attention away from it, but I don’t know that I’m doing anything deliberate.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling depressed most of the day?\n\nPatient: Yes. Most days it’s there for most of the day. It feels like the color has just drained out of everything, even if the house is busy around me. I’ll end up crying in the laundry room because I can’t stand anyone seeing how hopeless I feel.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had another period in your life when you felt depressed like this?\n\nPatient: Yes. I’ve had periods before where most days felt like that same heaviness—like everything was drained of color. I’d keep going because the house was busy, but I’d end up crying somewhere private, like the laundry room, because I didn’t want anyone to see how hopeless I felt.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period when you felt abnormally high, energized, or unusually irritable for several days?\n\nPatient: Yes. Earlier this month I had a stretch where I felt almost electrically upbeat, like I had all this energy. But then I’d snap at everyone over tiny things—like a medication cup being in the wrong place or the morning schedule changing. It didn’t feel like my usual self.\n\nDoctor: During that energized stretch, did you need much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes. I was only sleeping maybe three hours a night, and I’d still wake up before sunrise feeling wide awake and sharp, like I didn’t really need any more sleep.\n\nDoctor: During that energized stretch, did you feel unusually confident or powerful?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it powerful. I don’t really remember feeling like I was better than everyone or anything like that. Maybe more sure of myself than usual, but that part is hard to say.\n\nDoctor: During that energized stretch, were you more active than usual—starting lots of projects or doing much more than you normally would?\n\nPatient: Yes. For several days I just couldn’t sit still after work. I’d pace around the house, measure rooms, make lists, reorganize cabinets—stuff like that—and I’d keep doing it long after everyone else had gone to bed.\n\nDoctor: During that energized stretch, were you talking more than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t remember anyone telling me I was talking nonstop or anything like that. I may have been more chatty just because I felt keyed up, but I can’t say for sure.\n\nDoctor: During that energized stretch, did you do anything unusually risky or impulsive for you?\n\nPatient: Yes. I bought a bunch of things all at once—monitoring equipment, ramps, bedding, supplies—because at the time it felt like we absolutely needed them right then. Afterward I realized I’d put it all on the credit card, and we really can’t cover those charges.\n\nDoctor: During that energized stretch, did it cause major problems in your life—like you couldn’t function at work or home, or someone felt you needed emergency help?\n\nPatient: Yes, it’s caused real problems. I’ve been making mistakes at the clinic, which scares me, and I’ve been avoiding friends because I’m worried I’ll either snap at someone or just break down. At home, my husband has had to take over a lot—bills, meals, and watching out for me when my thoughts get really dark.\n\nDoctor: How many days did that energized and irritable stretch last?\n\nPatient: It lasted several days. I can’t remember the exact number, but for those days I couldn’t sit still after work—I was pacing around the house, measuring rooms, making lists, reorganizing cabinets, staying up doing that long after everyone else went to bed.\n\nDoctor: Did that energized and irritable period last a full week or longer?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it was a full week, no. It was several days, though, where after work I just couldn’t settle down—I kept pacing, measuring rooms, making lists, reorganizing things late after everyone else was in bed.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.
